1.  Introduction

1.1  Nesiga, the retraction of the word stress, has scarcely aroused any interest
among biblical scholars in this century , despite the fact that it is one of those
1
features of the Masoretic Text which is not well understood. It has, neverthe-
less, potential significance for a number of areas in which scholarly interest
continues active, so that it can reasonably be claimed that a new study fills
a need (if not a want) for students of the biblical text.

1.2  Reconstructions of the historical development of words in Hebrew regu-
larly assume that the position of the word stress has changed. The earliest di-
rect evidence of stress position is provided by the accentuation of the Bible, and
of other works used in the Jewish liturgy. This material provides, in fact, the
only direct evidence of stress position in any Semitic language prior to Euro-
pean descriptions of Arabic from the early seventeenth century. Consequently
the patterns of word stress shown in the most carefully preserved tradition, the
Standard Tiberian text of the Bible (MT), are of great significance for the his-
torical study of grammar. This does not apply only to the standard patterns;
variations in them are likely to reflect historical developments, so they have
particular importance. It has, indeed, been suggested that nesiga does not result
from the retraction of the word stress from the standard position, but represents
its retention in a position characteristic of a historically earlier period .2

1 See, however, the recent description in Yeivin, 1980, # 307310־.
2 This in suggested somewhat tentatively in Bergsträsser, 1918, # 22g, more definitely in Beer-Meyer,

1966, vol. i, p. 91.

— 9 —

#1-3

E. J. REVELL

1.3  Grammars inform us that stress position is, in a number of cases, the only
feature which distinguishes two different grammatical forms, as · בש ו  (root
הבש ) and , בש ל  (root בוש ).In some such cases, however, such as this
one, retraction of the stress in one of the forms can obliterate the distinction.
Consequently the claim that stress position is phonemic in Hebrew cannot be
maintained unless it can be shown that its retraction occurs under particular
conditions.

1.4  Attempts to discover, in biblical Hebrew poetry, a metre based on sylla-
bles or on word stress, usually involve the assumption that patterns of stress
and syllable structure typical of prose would be changed, where necessary, to
fit the metre. Nesiga, which clearly represents an adjustment of standard stress
patterns conditioned by the reading of the text, is much more common in
poetry than in prose . Whether or no nesiga can itself be related to any metre
original in this poetry, the way such adjustment is made in the received form
of the text is a potentially valuable guide to the way such adjustments were
made at a former stage of the language.

3

The Marking of Nesiga in the Tiberian Tradition

4

1.5  One reason for the lack of interest in nesiga may be the opinion that it
is not a stable feature of the Tiberian tradition. This study is based on L, the
Leningrad Codex . In the course of the study, some 600 cases (including all
those noted as unusual) were checked in the other manuscripts closely associa-
ted with the Ben Asher tradition. These are A (the Aleppo Codex), B (British
Museum MS Or. 4445, the Pentateuch), and C (the Cairo Codex of the Pro-
phets). Other material was also compared. The situation can be well illustrated
in the Latter Prophets, where A, C, and L are all extant, and the picture can

1070 possible cases. See #1.17.

against Gvar in thirteen other cases, and L,C,G,P agree against Gvar in one

1.9  This amply demonstrates the stability of nesiga in the Tiberian tradition.
P differs from L in only 31 of 160 cases (20 %), even though the accentuation
of P is not Standard Tiberian, as is,shown by the use of merka (see note 8).
The four best manuscripts, A, B, C, L, show no more variation in the other
parts of the Bible than they do in the Latter Prophets. The only definite variant
in nesiga in the material checked is that in Ez 23:40 (# 1.6), where nesiga is not
expected (see #1.12) and the reading of L may well result from scribal error.
In the other cases, the variation may be merely scribal, as the marking of maq-
qef need not reflect any difference in stress patterning.

1.10  Another case in which variation appears to occur is in ,ה'י O!>i}.BHS
and Dotan show two words in Ps 111:1, 112:1, 113:1, 135:1, 147:1, 148:1,
149:1, 150:1, as does Dotan in Ps 106:1, where BHS shows a single word. If
this is correct, the stroke under the he represents merka, and the word shows
nesiga. BHK shows a single word in all these cases, and this seems to be the
intention in A. In this case, the stroke under the he represents gacya. Other cases
are shown in L as two words joined by maqqef. A agrees, except in Ps 113:9,
117:2, where it appears to show one word. The scribe of L certainly did leave
a space (whether intentionally or no) in some of the cases listed. However it
is scarcely credible that the agreement between L and A in the marking of nesi-
ga, so very consistent elsewhere, should be so sharply breached in this particu-
lar pair of words. Consequently it seems likely that, whatever the intention of
the scribe, the intention of the pointing of L was to show a single word with
gacya, as in A, and not nesiga, where this is in question. Consequently these cas-
ses of
 are not treated as cases of nesiga in this study.

וללה הי

1.11  It is clear, then, that the best MSS of the Standard Tiberian Tradition
show minimal variation in the marking of nesiga, no more, in fact, than in any
other feature of the tradition . The study of this phenomenon presents no spe-
cial problems. Since L is the only one of the four «Ben Asher» manuscripts in
which the complete text is extant, it is the necessary choice as the basis for this
study.

9

9 In support of this it can be pointed out that, in the material checked, L and A differ more in the

letters of the text than in the marking of nesiga. Cf. Yeivin, 1980, # 30.

The Typical Situation in which Nesiga Occurs

1.12  Nesiga typically occurs in a word which would be, in normal context,
stressed on its final syllable, when it is joined by a conjuctive accent to a folio-
wing word stressed on its first syllable, that is, on the vowel following its first
consonant. The first word ends in two open syllables (------ CVCV), in two
open syllables separated by one with shewa (------- CVC3CV), Or in an open
syllable followed by a closed syllable (------ CVCVC). Stress is retracted to the
penultimate open syllable. Where the final syllable of the word is closed, its
vowel is almost always patah, segol, or sere. The only exceptions noted were10

Whatever the explanation for these cases of retraction of the stress from a clo-
sed syllable containing qames, or hireq, it is clear that they form an insignificant
proportion of the total number of cases. Consequently, in this study, «structure
typical for nesiga» refers to a word ending -------CVCV,-------CVC^V, or
-------CVCVC, which has final stress in ordinary contextual situations. Ä «si-
tuation typical for nesiga» is one in which a word of typical structure is joined
by a conjunctive accent to a word stressed on the vowel following its first conso-
nant.

Word Structure and Nesiga

1.13  Itis often suggested that nesiga fails to occur in words of particular struc-
ture, because their structure was not originally typical, but became so either
as a result of sound changes, or for other reasons'1. Stress is, however, retracted
to, or from, a syllable that was originally closed in a variety of forms, as
קתצ| (Gen 39:14), םתאב (Ez 36:22), היטענ (Jonai:!!) וק-וחב
(Prov. 8:27). It is also retracted to the vowel of a particle usually vowelled with
shewa, as  ה לע  (Isa 57:6), and in other surprising situations. Consequently all
forms which, according to the pointing of MT, have a structure typical for nesi-
ga, are included in this study. It is probable that features of word structure
not represented in MT do, in some cases, influence the development of nesiga.
The clearest example occurs in the pi’el infinitive form of  הכ ו
 (ft 3.6.1a). Other
possible examples are much less certain (see #2.7.11, 4.2, 4.3). In most cases
in which word structure has been suggested as the reason why nesiga does not
occur, its failure to occur is characteristic of the class of words as a whole, not
just those of a particular origin. It is true, for instance, that nesiga fails to occur
in words ending in י~ , or in the pronominal suffix  כ ם — , but this fact has
no intrinsic significance. Nesiga is extremely rare in words which end in a con-
sonant, unless they are verb forms, infinitives, or construct nouns.

1.14  A few words in which typical structure is abnormal are not included in
the study. The hollow root verb forms
ייאב  (Ez 30:4) and הלחו (Hosn:6)
show final stress which is fairly common in such forms, but is atypical.
The reasons for it are not fully understood . Final stress in

Accentuation and Nesiga

13

 לא

לעפמהמ

1.15  It is generally assumed that the accentuation of MT conditions the use
of features such as pausal forms and nesiga. However, even a superficial study
,of the relationship of accentuation and vowelling shows that this view can be
maintained only if numerous exceptions are admitted. The most striking ex-
ception in the present context is the single case in which nesiga occurs in a word
 (Nu 23:23). The view that the re-
with a disjunctive accent
ceived accentuation was fixed in the £ext after the vowelling had reached (more
or less) its present form is much easier to support
. Consequently this study
is based on the view that the accentuation does not condition the vowel and
stress patterns of MT. However the accentuation and the vowel and stress pat-
terns both reflect traditional ways of reading the same text. Consequently, the
accentuation is cited in this study as evidence of a particular analysis of the
text reflected by a tradition closely related to that reflected by the vowel and
stress patterns. No doubt the analysis reflected by both traditions was often the
same, but it is quite certain that it was not always so.

14

1.16  On this basis, the use of a conjunctive accent on the first of the two words
in question is given as one of the characteristics of the situation typical for nesi-
ga. Where the two words were sufficiently closely joined to give rise to nesiga
in the form of tradition reflected by the vowel and stress patterns, they are (in
all cases but one) sufficiently closely joined in the tradition reflected by the ac-
centuation to require a conjunctive accent. Where such an accent is used, nesiga
can occur, but the accent has no relevance whatever for the question of whether
nesiga does occur in any given word. The view that certain combinations of
accents prevent the occurrence of nesiga is demostrably invalid. No combina-
tion of accents inhibits the development of nesiga even to the extent that nesiga
fails to occur with that combination in a majority of the possible cases. The
closest possibilites are situations in which the second of the two words has geresh

the individual accents were used in the text. It does not affect the question of the date (relative or absolute)
of the introduction of the vowel signs and the accent signs, nor does it affect the question of the age of the

#1-16

E. J. REVELL

(where nesiga occurs in 50% of the possible cases)15, a conjunctive (51 %) or
tifha (57 %). In the first two cases, the combinations tend to occur in situations
in which the words forming the typical situation are closely related to what
follows. It is this relationship (which the accents reflect, but do not create)
which inhibits nesiga. The reason why nesiga is so much less common before tifha
than would be expected from its status in the hierarchy of disjunctive accents,
is no doubt the fact that the use of tifha before atnah and silluq is a musical re-
quirement, independent of the syntactic/semantic structure of the passage16.
The occurrence of nesiga is determined by the syntactic/semantic structure of the
passage as analyzed in the tradition reflected by the stress and vowel patterns.
It is not affected by the musical requirements governing the accentuation.

1.17  Nesiga occurs in all possible cases (in the situations described in note 15)
before a few accents, but only where the possible cases are also few. (The figure
of 57 % for tifha is based on 188 possible cases). Thus nesiga always occurs be-
forepazer (2 cases), telisha gedola (1), coleh we-yored (3), revia mugrash (7), and legar-
meh (5). The last three accents are those of the Three Books; it is no accident
that these account for most of the cases. Nesiga is significantly more common
in the Three Books than in the Twenty-One (see note 3). This is due in part
to the fact that clauses in the Three Books are typically short, for nesiga is most
likely to occur where words forming the typical situation stand at the end of
a clause . However nesiga is also more common within clauses in the Three
Books than in the Twenty-One, suggesting that the Three Books were read in

17

the conjunctive is always azla. The cases showing nesiga are Lev 15:13, Dt 17:18, 21:13, 24:1, 3, Jud 16:5,

to the development of nesiga. The different forms of accentuation used for the
Three Books and for the Twenty-One may well reflect the same fact. It is, never-
theless, demonstrably true that the significance of the accentuation for nesiga
is no greater in the Three Books than in the Twenty-One.

Syntax, Semantics, and Nesiga

18

1.18  As former scholars have observed , nesiga is most common where the
typical situation is formed by a verb and its following subject or modifier, or
by two nouns in a construct phrase. That is, nesiga is most easily induced where
the two words are closely related syntactically. Among such cases, nesiga is most
common where the two words end a clause, and most common of all where
the second of the two words has atnah or silluq. These are also the positions in
which pausal forms most commonly occur. Nesiga, like the pausal form, is a
phenomenon characteristic of the ends of speech units. Nesiga is, however, rela-
tively more common than are pausal forms. That is, nesiga occurs not only at
the end the «clause-level» speech units marked by pausal forms, but also at
the end of their subdivisions, or «phrase-level» speech units. The obvious as-
sumption is that the changes in stress position and vowel pattern characteristic
of nesiga reflect, as do those of pausal forms, the influence, on stress position
and vowelling, of the intonation patterns which delimit those speech units and
their subdivisions. These intonation patterns are those characteristic of the for-
mal reading of the text in the tradition reflected by the stress and vowel pat-
terns. Such formal reading is designed to present the meaning of the text
clearly to the hearer. Hence the relationship of these terminal phenomena (ne-
siga and pausal forms) and the syntactic/semantic structure of the context in
which they occur.

1.19  The detailed description of the occurrence of nesiga in # 2-7 is an attempt
to demonstrate the validity of this hypothesis by showing the relationship of the
individual cases of nesiga to their context. This is done in the following frame-
work.

(i)  The understanding of a phenomenon of this sort requires the consideration
both of the cases in which it does occur, and of those in which it could occur,

18 See Praetorius, 1897, p. 353־θ·

— 17 —

# 1.19

E. J. REVELL

but does not. Consideration of cases in which nesiga does not occur is confined
to examples of the typical situation as defined in # 1.12, since the proportion
of cases occurring under other conditions is negligible. The intention is to in-
elude all typical situations of this sort. Cases in which stress is not marked on
the first word, as where it has maqqef, or telisha qetanna, cannot, of course, be
included. A few cases in which penultimate stress occurs in situations different
from the typical, though similar, are discussed in # 8.

(ii)  It is assumed that nesiga occurs where two words were closely joined in
speech, and were followed by a pause at the end of a speech unit. Nesiga fails
to occur where the relationship of either of the two words to their context is
such as to prevent their being closely joined, or such as to prevent their being
followed by a pause. It is assumed that these relationships which affect nesiga
may exist between the words, phrases, and clauses within the larger speech
units, but not across the boundaries of such units. These larger speech units
are those delimited by the intonation patterns which give rise to pausal forms.
Since many words do not appear in distinct pausal forms, these cannot be used
to identitify the boundaries of such units. Consequently the accents silluq and
atnah, or (in the Three Books) coleh we-yored are taken as marking the boundaries
beyond which semantic relationship does not affect nesiga. The units (verse-hal-
ves) which they delimit are referred to in the study as «major units». About
20 % of pausal forms occur within major units, and occasionally major units
end with contextual forms, so that this system, though the best which can be
achieved, lacks something in accuracy .19

1.20  The situations typical for nesiga are categorized below, first according to
the syntactical relationship which joins the two words, and then according to
their relationship to the surrounding context, as described for each category.
An attempt is then made to isolate and describe those features which dis-
tinguish the cases in which nesiga occurs in each category from those in which
it does not
. This description is limited to features of structure, or syntactic
or semantic relationship, which can be objectively described. Often several

20

19 Cf. the conclusions of Kasher, 1985, on the existence of more than one tradition of verse division.

20 In the paragraph describing each category, a verse-reference is given for each case assigned to that
category. The actual word in question can be found, if necessary, from the index. It should be noted that,
in the illustrative examples, the qere form is given (except in the case of the divine name) with no indication,
since the reading tradition is the subject of this study, not the written text. Apart from this, the spelling
of L is followed as a matter of course.

— 18 —

NESIGA

#1.20

such features can be seen, in which case the one or two most consistent with
the common patterns are mentioned. These features are summarized at the end
of the chapter on each syntactic category. Only the features which appear to
affect nesiga in a consistent way are included in these summaries, and conside-
red to represent definite results. Features not included in the summaries may
appear to be without significance because they are rare, but no doubt they are
often simply irrelevant. The development of nesiga is undoubtedly conditioned
by the lexical relationship of items in the context, and this would usually be
the dominant factor where the features included in the summaries are not pre-
sent. Unfortunately, however, these lexical factors are not accessible to objecti-
ve description.

1.21  The conclusion of this study is that nesiga is a variation automatically
conditioned by the intonation contours marking the ends of phrase-level
speech units both within, and at the ends of, clause-level units. As stated above,
the occurrence of nesiga requires both that the two words forming the typical
situation occur at the end of a speech unit, and that they be closely joined to
each other. In the syntactic categories in which nesiga most commonly occurs,
the correlation of nesiga with syntactic and other semantic factors is quite clear,
and it is assumed that the close-joining represents the normal spoken reflection
of the syntactic bond. In other categories, where nesiga is distinctly less com-
mon, it appears likely that the necessary close-joining in speech occurs only
as a result of the natural rhythm of speech. This, of course, is also inaccessible
to objective description, but the effect of speech rhythm on segmentation, etc.
in living language is well known, so the assumptions that it affected the biblical
reading traditions is wholly reasonable. In a very few cases the conditions un-
der which nesiga occurs can be clearly stated, but, for no visible reason, nesiga
does not always occur under those conditions (cf. #6.7). This statement may
appear to admit, for the theory presented here, the same inconsistency used
as an argument for rejecting the theory that nesiga is conditioned by the accents
(# 1.16). In the case of that theory, however, the supposed conditioning factors
are visible in the text. According to the theory presented here, the conditioning
factors can only be imperfectly reconstructed, since they are features of the li-
ving speech of people dead many centuries. This being so, it is remarkable that,
in the attempt to support this theory in the following chapters, so few cases
need be dismissed as anomalous.

— 19 —

2.  Nesiga in Verb Forms

2.1  The term «verb form» includes only finite verbs; infinitives and partici-
pies are treated separately. These finite verbs (perfect, imperfect, and impera-
five forms) always occur in verbal clauses, of which, of they form the characteris-
tic constituent. The semantic relationships which determine the development
of nesiga in a verb form must be described in terms of the other constituents
of the clause, the structure of the clause, and the relationship of that clause
to the other clauses in the context. The following paragraphs provide some ne-
cessary background to such description.

2.2  The Constituents of the Verbal Clause.
V The verbal constituent, the basis of the clause, usually consists of a verb
form alone. A preceding negative is considered as part of the verbal con-
stituent. Verb forms with pronominal suffixes are treated separately in #2.47;
they are not included in the main description below. The verbal constituent
may act as a clause on its own, but clauses often include other constituents,
as follows.
N Nominal. These are nouns (including participles and infinitives not pre-
ceded by a preposition) or noun substitutes in the form of pronouns, noun
phrases, clauses introduced by יפ or רשא , or clauses representing quoted
speech. They function as
Ns the subject of the clause, or as
No a nominal constituent with other function: object, subjective completion
or modifying the verb in some other way.

#2.2

pN Prepositional. A preposition followed by a nominal structure (excluding
infinitives). Where a verb and a pN constituent form a situation typical for
nesiga, the nominal component of the prepositional constituent is always a pro-
nominal suffix.
pl Infinititival. A preposition followed by an infinitive and any modifiers it
may have.
אנ The «particle of entreaty» which, where it occurs in the material described,
always follows the verb immediately.
Av Adverbial. This category includes nouns with the adverb-forming affix,
which

may act as constituents of a clause following the verb. Some other particles
which precede the verb are treated as constituents of the clause for present pur-

are named individually.

2.4  The Structure of Verbal Clauses.

As is well known, the constituents described above are usually arranged
according to a well defined order, with the verb first. However, any constituent
may precede the verb. In some cases a constituent preceding the verb is repre-
sented after it by an anaphoric element (a pronoun or adverb). Such a con-
stituent is referred to as «extraposed»; it does not form part of the basic struc-
ture of the clause (as casus pendens). Nevertheless, it does have a close semantic
relationship to the following clause, and is therefore included in the category
of constituents preceding the verb. A few particles not listed in # 2.2 are simi-
lary classed as constituents where they precede the verb of a clause. These are

is in question, it is given in terms of the number of constituents following the

2.5  The Interrelationship of Clauses.

(i)  Clauses which form (part of) a constituent of another clause, that is, clau-
ses which act as noun substitutes, are treated separately (#2.39 and following),
except in the case of clauses representing quoted speech. The speech is con-
sidered as No in relation to the verb of speaking, but the clauses forming it
are analyzed with the other independent clauses.

(ii)  Where a pair of parallel clauses requires the same verb, it may not be
repeated in the second clause, but is «understood» there. Where this occurs,
the verb is, objectively speaking, used with two parallel sets of constituents.
The second (verbless) set of constituents in such cases is referred to as a «depen-
dent» clause (because dependent on the verb of the preceding clause).

(iii)  The relationship of one clause to another may be indicated by particles.
In the conditional sentence, the relationships so marked is close and consistent.
Such sentences are formed (in the material described) by a protasis intro-
duced by  א ם  «if», followed by an apodosis (the result clause). The relation-
ship marked by other subordinating conjunctions, as י S «because», 3ו
«lest», appears to be less consistent in terms of its effect on nesiga.

(iv)  Relationship between clauses is also marked by anaphora: pronouns or
adverbs representing a constituent, or part of a constituent, of a preceding
clause. Other internal features of clauses may also mark significant relation-
ship, but reference to these is avoided, where possible, in an effort to present
as objective a classification as possible.

(v)  Since the major unit is a semantic unit, the position of a clause within
it must indicate its relationship to the surrounding clauses. A clause which ends
such a unit must be related to what precedes; a clause which begins such a
unit must be related to what follows.

2.6  The material in the description below is arranged according to the fea-
tures appearing to condition the development of nesiga which are most easily
described objectively. Clauses are initially divided into three categories: clauses
in which the verb is the first constituent, clauses in which the verb is not the
first constituent, and clauses which form (part of) a constituent of another

— 24 —

NESIGA

#2.6

clause. Within each category, the clauses are classified by length, according to
whether the verb is followed by one constituent, by two one-word constituents,
by two constituents of which the second is more than one word, or by more
than two constituents. The clauses of each type are then grouped according
to their position in the major unit: at its end, at its beginning, within it (neither
at the end at the beginning), or forming the major unit (which is then made
up of a single clause). The occurrence of nesiga in each of these groups is then
described, and an attempt is made to find features of structure or relationship
by which those clauses which show nesiga are destinguished from those which
do not. Following this description, those features which most generally corre-
late with nesiga are summarized (# 2.48 fl).

2.12  Where the clause stands at the beginning of a major unit, nesiga occurs

followed by pN).

an object.

subject, and the two form a conditional sentence (1S 2:25, Jer 23:24), or the

to occur in the first of two parallel clauses in which the last constituent is the

2.14  Where the clause forms a major unit, nesiga occurs in 16 of 31 cases
(52 %). Its occurrence is clearly conditioned by the composition of the clause
in some cases (nesiga always occurs before pN). In other cases there is little dif-
ference in the conditions under which nesiga does and does not occur.

(i)

In the combination V — Ns

(a)  Nesiga fails to occur where the verb is followed by both subject and

object (Mic 5:4, Nah 3:13).

Nesiga occurs in this situation in Ps 71:8, Job 39:9, possibly reflecting the
fact that the form of reading characteristic of the Three Books was more con-
ducive to nesiga than that of the Twenty-One.

(b)  Where the verb is followed by Ns and pN, nesiga fails to occur in
the second half of the verse in the Twenty-One Books (Jud 21:17, 1K 17:7,
Jer 4:10, Hab 3:5, Zech 11:1).

Nesiga occurs in the first half of the verse in Nu 20:2, 1C 21:11, and in
the second half in Job 15:19. If the first half of the verse was read with a termi-
nal contour somewhat different from that of the second (as is suggested by the
Babylonian accentuation for that tradition), the difference might affect the
ocurrence of nesiga (as it appears that the different forms of reading used in
the Three and the Twenty-One Books did).

(ii)

In the combination V — No
Nesiga fails to occur in Lev 19:35, Dt 32:3, and Ps 83:6.
Nesiga occurs in Ps 83:11, where (unlike the other cases) the verb is a

form of היה, and the following noun its «subjective completion».

(iii)

In the combination V — pN
Nesiga occurs in Ex 25:8, Nu 3:12, 8:14, 2S 19:24, 2K 1:17, Isa 8:15, Jer

30:22, 32:38, Ps 106:31, Job 39:17.

(iv)

In the combination V — Av
Nesiga fails to occur in Gen 38:26, 1S 27:4, 2S 2:28, Hos 2:19, Zech 2:16.

The adverb is דוע in all cases.

III  . The Verb is Followed by Two Constituents, the Second of More

2.15  Where the clause ends a major unit, nesiga occurs in 30 of 44 cases
(68 %). Nesiga generally occurs where the relationship to the preceding clause
is not close, but the composition of the clause also has an effect (nesiga is more
likely to occur where the verb is followed by pN).

(i)

In the combination V — Ns
Nesiga fails to occur where the clause is not closely related to what pre-
cedes in Ex 12:4 (the clause acts as an apodosis, but the two constituents fol-
lowing the verb form a compound subject different from that of the preceding
protasis) and 1S 11:13 (quoted speech following an introduction).

Nesiga occurs where the clause is relatively closely related to what pre-
cedes in Ex 10:21 (the verb is jussive continuing an imperative) and Isa 66:8
(in the second of a pair of alternative questions).

(ii)

In the combination V — No
Nesiga does not occur in Isa 5:14, Qph 3:13. In both, the preceding clause

has the same subject.

(iii)

In the combination V — pN
(a)  Nesiga fails to occur where the V — pN combination introduces
quoted speech, and the preceding clause consists of יהיו. plus a temporal
phrase (Gen 20:13).

Nesiga occurs in other cases in which the clause has a different subject
from the preceding (Jud 9:54, 2K 8:14), and in all cases in which the subject
is the same (Gen 19:5, 24:60, 47:18, Jud 11:2, 16:5, 18:3, 1S 10:19, 12:12, 1K
21:6, 2K 2:23, Ez 16:6, 26:17).

(b)

In other situations, nesiga fails to occur where the subject of the
clause is different from the preceding, and the second of the two constituents
following the verb is prepositional (Lam 1:22), or is a short construct phrase

#2.23

THE VERB IS PRECEDED BY A CONSTITUENT OF THE CLAUSE

I. The Verb is Followed by One Constituent

2.23  Where the clause ends a major unit, nesiga occurs in 61 of 65 cases

VERB FORMS WITH PRONOMINAL SUFFIX

2.47  A verb form with pronominal suffix and the word following it form a
situation typical for nesiga in a small number of cases. Nesiga occurs only where
the suffix consists of a single vowel. Consequently nesiga does not occur in forms
with the 2ms suffix η- in a typical situation in Gen 46:3, 1S 26:19, 2K 4:29,
Ps 5:5, Prov 27:2, or in forms with the 2fs suffix 51־ in 1K 1:12, Mic 4:9, Nah
3:15, Rut 3:13, or in a form with the 3mpl suffix ם.ד in 2C 25:14. Where the
suffix consists of a single vowel, nesiga occurs in 6 of 8 cases (75 %)·

(i)  Nesiga fails to occur where the verb is followed by a single constituent, and
the clause is subordinate, ending a pN element extraposed before the clause
it relates to (Dt 22:26, cf. # 2.40.111).

— 50 —

NESIGA

# 2.47.1

Nesiga occurs where the verb is followed by a single constituent in other
circumstances in Job 31:29, where the verb is clause-initial, and in Jer 23:9,
Amos i:ii12, Job 13:28, 28:7, where the clause is an unmarked relative. In
all five cases, the clause ends a major unit (cf. # 2.7, 2.39).

) Nesiga also fails to occur where the verb is followed by two one-word

(ii
constituents in 2S 18:11, where the verb is preceded by an interrogative, and
followed by two adverbs (cf. #2.27, and, for the interrogative, ft 2.34).

Nesiga occurs in Nu 23:27, where a clause-initial verb is followed by two
pN elements (cf. #2.11). Both here and in 2S 18:11, the clause ends a major
unit.

As far as can be determined from this limited information, nesiga in these
forms is conditioned in the same way as in forms with no pronominal suffix,
save that, where the verb has a suffix, nesiga occurs only in forms ending
—vcv.

also appears to occur in הרמחתו (Ex 2:3, no rafe in L), and המוורו (Jer 20:17, rafe in L).

2.49  This table demonstrates clearly the effect of the factors which governed
the presentation of the material (# 2.6).

(i)  A percentage figure in group A is generally lower than the figure for the
corresponding category (line and colum) in group B. Thus nesiga is more likely
to occur where the verb is preceded by a constituent of the clause than where
it is not. That is, close relationship to what precedes is conducive to the de-
velopment of nesiga. The significant exceptions occur where the verb is followed
by two constituents (lines 2 and 3), for the reasons given in (iii) below.

(ii)  The figure in line 1 is usually the highest in each group in any column.
Thus nesiga is most likely to occur where the verb is followed by a single
constituent. That is, a significant division following the word after the verb
is conducive to nesiga. The significant exceptions occur in colums II and III,
reflecting the significance of the position of the clause in the major unit (iv
below).

(iii)  The figure in line 2 is usually the lowest in each group in any column.
Thus nesiga is least likely to occur where verb is followed by two short con-
stituents. It seems probable that, where verb is clause-initial (group A), such
clauses would be treated as a unit, with no division following the word after
the verb (cf. ii above). Longer clauses would usually be divided. Where the
verb was clause-initial, and followed by two constituents, the second of more
than one word, the division would usually follow the word after the verb, divi-
ding the clause into two more or less equal parts, and giving rise to nesiga. Where
the verb was preceded by a constituent, and followed by two short constituents,
a division of the clause into two more or less equal parts would require a divi-
sion after the verb, so that the association between the verb and the following
word would not be close enough to give rise to nesiga.

— 52 —

NESIGA

#2.49

In group A, the figure in column 1 is significantly higher than that in
(iv)
column 2 in lines 1 and 4. Thus in short or long clauses, nesiga is more likely
to occur at the end of a major unit than at the beginning. A two-word clause
at the end of a major unit would typically be treated as a unit, and so show
nesiga. A short clause at the beginning of a major unit would typically be joined
to what followed, which would prevent the development of nesiga. In a long
clause at the end of a major unit, relationship with what preceded would typi-
cally result in a division towards the beginning of the clause, often after the
word after the verb, giving rise to nesiga. At the beginning of a major unit, where
the relationship is to what follows, such a clause would be likely to be divided
at a later point, so that nesiga would not occur. A three-word clause (line 2)
would typically be treated as a unit in any position (see iii above), so that posi-
tion at the beginning or at the end of a major unit has little effect on the occu-
rrence of nesiga. A longer three-constituent clause, where closely related to
what precedes, would typically be divided after the verb (see iii above), and
so is less likely to show nesiga at the end of a major unit.

In group A, the figures in colum III, representing clauses standing within
(v)
a major unit, which may be related either to what precedes or to what follows,
fall between those in columns I and II in lines 1 and 4, but are higher than
either in lines 2 and 3, presumably for reasons similar to the arguments in (iv)
above. The figures in column IV show no consistent relationship to those in
the other columns, since where the clause forms a major unit, the development
of nesiga must depend on internal factors, not on external relationships.

(vi)  Where the verb does not stand first in the clause, it is inevitably closely
related to what precedes it, so that position within the major unit has little in-
fluence on the development of nesiga. Consequently in group B, the figures in
the different columns do not show the same relationship to each other as in
group A.

2.50  The factors represented in the table in #2.48 are not the only ones
which condition the development of nesiga, but the significance of other fac-
tors must be demonstrated by reference to the descriptions of the individual
categories. This results from the way the material was presented; it does not
indicate that these other factors are necessarily less significant than those re-
presented in the table. These other factors can be grouped under the hea-
dings «Relationship between Clauses», «Composition of the Clause», and
«Rhythmic Factors».

— 53 —

#2.51

E. J. REVELL

Relationship between Clauses

2.51  Close relationship between clauses which is marked by anaphora has the
same effect as the types of relationship discussed in # 2.49.

(i)  Close relationship to the following clause prevents nesiga from occurring
in a short clause at the beginning of a major unit (# 2.8, 2.12, 2.24), or within
one (#2.9, 2.13, 2.40).

(ii)  Close relationship to what precedes tends to induce nesiga in a short three
constituent clause at the end of a major unit (# 2.11), and in a two-constituent
clause within a major unit (#2.9), and perhaps elsewhere (#2.42). However
particularly close relationship to what precedes prevents the development of
Nesiga where the verb is followed by a single constituent at the end of a major
unit (#2.7, 2.23) and perhaps in a longer clause (#2.15). No doubt such re-
lationship prevented the close association between the verb and the following
word neccessary for the development of nesiga.

(iii)  The lack of close relationship to what precedes correlates with the oc-
currence of nesiga in longer three-constituent clauses at the end of a major unit
(#2.15). Such a clause would typically be divided after the word after the verb,
inducing nesiga in the verb. Presumably close relationship to the preceding
would tend to result in a division immediately after the verb, preventing the
development of nesiga (see # 2.49.iv).

2.52  Semantic relationship is also marked in other ways. Where this is done
by particles, the most consistent effect on nesiga in seen in conditional sentences.
The close relationship of the two parts of the sentence to each other may pre-
vent nesiga in the protasis where it would be expected (# 2.12.ib, iii), and may
permit the development of nesiga in the apodosis where it would not otherwise
occur (# 2.x i.ii, 2.13.1, but not 2.15.1). The relationship between a subordinate
temporal clause and the following main clause also appears to prevent the de-
velopment of nesiga (#2.12.ib). There is no good evidence that the relationship
indicated by other particles had a significant influence.

Other phenomena no doubt also reflect semantic relationships which would
have the same effect on nesiga as those marked by anaphora and particles. An
example is the «ordinary narrative» relationships of verbs in successive clauses, as
opposed to «other» relationship (#2.n.iib). However few semantic relationships
of this sort are likely to be common enough to show up in a general survey such
as this; more possibilities may be revealed by a study of individual examples.

— 54 —

NESIGA

#2.53

The Composition and Structure of the Clause

2.53  The composition of the clause clearly affects the development of nesiga
in that nesiga almost always occurs before #2) אנ.g.v, 2.1 i.v, 2.15.V, 2.18.v;
the only exception is # 2.12.v). This very likely reflects the fact that this particle
was typically closely joined to a preceding verb by the natural rhythm of
speech. Nesiga occurs before pN much more frequently than before nominal,
infinitival, or adverbial constituents, and nesiga occurs before  ש ם  more com-
monly than before other adverbs (#2.8.iv, 2.11.iv, 2.15.1V, 2.18.iv, 2.19.Ü,
2.20.ii). The important factor here is presumably the relationship marked by
the anaphoric element. It does not appear that the presence or position of other
types of constituent has any consistent effect on the occurrence of nesiga, despite
the frequent reference to them in the description. If these references do reflect
any significant factor, it is probably a matter of lexical relationship specific to
the clause, not of syntactic relationship typical of clauses generally.

2.54  Where a verb is followed by two constituents, the length of the second
is clearly significant for the development of nesiga, as noted in #2.49.111. The
discussion there concerns clauses in which this constituent is a single word, but
it appears that two short words in close syntactical relationship may have the
same effect as a single word, at least where acting as the second of two con-
stituents following the verb in a clause which stands at the end of a major unit
(# 2.15.iiib), or forms one (# 2.18,1, iii). Length may also be a significant factor
in the second constituent following the verb in a long clause ending or forming
a major unit (#2.19, 2.22.iii). Where the first two constituents following the
verb are short, they are likely to be treated as forming a three-word unit with
the verb, preventing the development of nesiga in the verb. The length of a
constituent preceding the verb may also have a significant effect on nesiga
(#2.23, 2.24.1, 2.26.1, 2.27, 2.30.111). Where such a constituent was short, its
effect on the rhythmic factors determining the point at which the clause was
divided would be relatively weak.

Rhythmic Factors

2.55  In the above discussion, it has been assumed that, as a general rule, a
clause of more than three words would be divided into two more or less equal
parts. This is assumed to be a feature of the linguistic rhythm characteristic
of the formal reading of the biblical text in the tradition reflected by the pheno­

— 55 —

#2.55

E. J. REVELL

mena discussed. Another feature of this tradition appears to have been a parti-
cular maner of reading the Three Books which was more conducive to the de-
velopment of nesiga than was the manner of reading the Twenty-One (#1.17).
This may have affected the development of nesiga in verb forms in a few cases
(# 2.1 i.i, 2.14.1a). It is also possible that the manner of reading the two halves
of a verse in the Twenty-One Books differed in a way which affected the de-
velopment of nesiga (# 2.14.1b).

Another feature affecting nesiga which probably arises from natural
speech rhythms is the tendency to treat items in pairs, a tendency which ap-
pears, in some cases, to have a greater effect on nesiga than does semantic re-
lationship. Where nesiga fails to occur in the first of a pair of clauses, there is
usually a close semantic relationship between the two (as #2.g.iiib, 2.12.1, iv,
2.13.111). In the case in #2.13.11, however, there is a stronger relationship
between the pair of clauses than between the protasis which they form and the
following apodosis. Where nesiga occurs in the second of a pair of clauses, it
may be as closely related to the following as to the preceding (in many of the
cases in #2.9 the clause is second of a group of three with the same subject),
or it may have no close relationship to the preceding (# 2.g.iid, e, iiid, e). Thus
it is the tendency to treat clauses in pairs (assisted, no doubt, by lexical factors),
which provides the bond of relationship which induces nesiga.

2.56  Even though the development of nesiga can be shown to be influenced
in such a variety of ways, there are cases in which nesiga occurs in one verb
form, but not in another, in situations which seem virtually identical. It is sig-
nificant that these occur where a clause with an initial verb followed by two
or more constituents forms a major unit (#2.14, 2.18, 2.22). In this situation
nesiga cannot be conditioned by what precedes the verb, or by what follows
the clause. Similary, in a fourth situation in which this occurs, where a long
clause with an initial verb begins a major unit (# 2.20), the verb is not related
to what precedes, and the length of the clause would nullify the effect on the
verb of any relationships to what follows. In these categories, nesiga occurs where
the verb is followed by אנ , and usually where it is followed by pN or by
םש .In other cases, the development of nesiga must be conditioned by the
lexical factors relating the constituents. Such factors are, of course, present in
all clauses, and no doubt account for many of the exceptions to common pat-
terns. In the four categories mentioned here, however, the development of nesi-
ga is conditioned only by such lexical factors, at least where the verb is followed
by Ns, No, pl or Av (other than םש ). These lexical factors are not for-

— 56 —

NESIGA

#2.56

mally marked. Consequently no clear pattern emerges from the above analysis,
which depends on factors which can be objectively described.

Some of the lack of pattern in these categories is also no doubt to be ac-
counted for by the fact that, where a division must be made (for rhythmic rea-
sons), but would be equally appropriate (semantically) in more than one place,
it is likely to be placed differently in different examples of the same text. This
is demonstrably true of the accentuation of different biblical manuscripts (see
note 16 on # 1.16). It is the probable reason why nesiga occurs in one of two
identical clauses, but not in the other (#2.18.1). Since only one such case has
been noted, however, it cannot affect the general conclusion that the variation
represented by nesiga is automatic, and is generally conditioned by semantic
factors.

— 57 —

3. Nesiga in Infinitives

3.1  The term «infinitive» here refers to the infinitive construct. Nesiga does
not occur in the three cases in which absolute infinitives stand in a typical si-
tuation (Isa 59:4, Jer 4:18, Ez 23:30).

The infinitive construct may be modified by any of the structures listed
in # 2.2 as constituents of the verbal clause. Modifiers usually follow the infini-
tive in the same order as that in which they follow the verb, but occasionally
they precede it. An infinitive phrase usually modifies a preceding word. This
is the case with those described below unless otherwise indicated.

Pronouns may be suffixed to infinitive forms. Nesiga occurs in such forms
only where the suffix consists of a single vowel, and so fails to occur in forms
with the 2ms suffix fl- which stand in a typical situation in 1S 10:5, 15:7,
17:52, 2S 5:25, Isa 49:6. Forms with a suffix consisting of a vowel are included
with the forms with no suffix in the lists below.

3.2  Where the word following the infinitive stands at the end of a major unit,
nesiga occurs in 14 of 19 cases (74%). The failure of nesiga appears to be due
either to the composition of the phrase, or to the structure of the infinitive.

3.6 The factors which appear to condition the development of nesiga in infini-
fives can be summarized as follows.

(i)  Nesiga occurs where the constituent following the infinitive includes an
anaphoric element (pN in # 3.2, 3.3, 3.4.Ü, דבש in # 3.5.111).

Nesiga fails to occur in this situation:
(a)  where the infinitive is api’el form from 3.4 ,3.3 ,3.2 #) . והכ.Ü).
(b)  where the phrase ends a constituent which modifies a following

verb (#3.4.11).

(c)  where the infinitive is followed by more than two modifiers, of
which the first two are single words, where the second does not from a com-
pound with what follows (# 3.5.Ü, iii).

(d)  where the pl phrase is particularly closely related to what follows

(Est 2:9 in #3.4.11).

NESIGA

#3.6

(ü) Where the infinitive is followed by a noun or by a non-anaphoric adverb,
nesiga does not occur even at the end of a clause (#3.2, 3.3) or elsewhere
(# 3.4.1, 3.5.1), with the following exceptions:

(a)

In Nu 24:22 (# 3.2) in an anomaly (the noun does not modify the
infinitive). Nesiga here presumably reflects the fact that the noun was closely
joined to the preceding infinitive due to the rhythm of speech.
In 2S 23:17 (# 3.3) in an oath formula.
In some cases in which the infinitive is followed by two or more mo-
difiers (#3.5.1). In cases of this sort in the Three Books, the development of
nesiga appears to be conditioned by the semantic relationship of the following
noun to the infinitive.

(b)
(c)

3.7 Thus the most significant factor conditioning the development of nesiga
in infinitives is relationship to what precedes or follows. Other factors, such as
the composition of the infinitive phrase, and its position in the major unit, also
probably had some influence, but this cannot be demonstrated clearly. Where
the infinitive is related to what precedes by a following anaphoric modifier,
the development of nesiga is only prevented by the structure of the infinitive
(# 3.6.1a), or by the close relationship of the phrase to what follows (# 3.6.1b,
c, d). Despite the importance of an anaphoric element in the following word,
the presence of a pronoun suffixed to the infinitive has no appreciable effect
on the development of nesiga, as is the case with verb forms. This shows clearly
that nesiga does not develop unless the two-word phrase forming the typical
situation is closely related to what precedes. Where only the first word of the
pair is so related, the development of nesiga may be inhibited (cf. # 2.49.111).

— 63 —

4.  Nesiga in Construct Nouns

4.1  This chapter deals with the cases in which a construct phrase provides
a situation typical for nesiga. The second of the two words forming the situation
is usually a noun in this case, but noun substitutes of any type may fill this
position, and it is occasionally taken by a pN element. The first word of the
two is typically a noun in the construct state, but prepositions capable of showing
nesiga are included here . Nouns are included where they can be identified as
construct either by features of their form, or by analysis of their syntax. As a
result, participles are included only where they are construct in form, since
where this is not the case it is not possible to determine whether the relationship
to a following noun is nominal (construct) or verbal.

1

The Construct Phrase Stands at the End of a Major Unit

4.2  Where a two-word construct phrase stands at the end of a major unit,
nesiga occurs in 183 of 189 cases (97 %). No very plausible reason can be sug-
gested for the failure of nesiga to occur in the six cases where it does not, which
are:

The following features can be offered as possibly connected with the failure of
nesiga to occur. In Lev 23:2 and 23:37, the end of the major unit is not the
end of the clause. In Nu 18:30, the phrase in question is preceded by a parallel
one in which the same word in construct does not show nesiga (see #4.7). In
Dt 4:12, the phrase is sometimes interpreted as independent, not as modifying
what precedes it, and this may have been the case in the form of the tradition
represented by nesiga. In Ps 118:17, the failure of nesiga may be an example
of the fact that nesiga never occurs before הי , or of the fact that final stress
is sometimes required before a monosyllable at the end of a major unit (see
#2.7.111). Finally, the case in Dan 8:1g is unique in that the construct phrase
functions as a clause and as a major unit, which may well have resulted in un-
usual treatment affecting the development of nesiga.

It might be suggested that the development of nesiga was prevented in

The development of nesiga might have been prevented in this case by the fact
that the penultimate syllable of the construct form was originally closed, or by
the fact that the phrase was treated as an independent structure (cf. Jerome),
and not as modifying what precedes. There is, however, no independent sup-
port for either suggestion.

Nesiga occurs in this category in Gen 4:2, 46:32, Ex 2:3, Dt 4:25, 32:13,

The Construct Phrase Stands at the End of a Constituent

4.4  Where the construct phrase stands at the end of a constituent, but not
at the end of a clause, nesiga occurs in 82 of 99 cases (84%). Nesiga fails to
occur where the phrase is closely related to what follows. This occurs where
the phrase acts as, or ends, the first part of a verbless clause, where it stands
immediately before the verb it modifies, or where it is first of a pair of parallel
phrases.

2

4.5  Nesiga fails to occur in the first part of a verbless clause  where it is a
phrase of two or three words, and the clause is completed by two or more
words, regardless of syntactical function. Thus nesiga fails to occur in Mic 7:6
in a clase of two two-word parts, in 1S 24:14 in a two-word phrase extraposed
before two words acting as subject and predicate, in Gen 47:3 and 2S 17:8 in
a two-word phrase acting as subject in the one case, and predicate in the other,
which is followed by one word (acting as predicate or subject), which is fol-
lowed by modifiers of the initial phrase. Nesiga also fails to occur in a three-
word phrase in a similar structure in

Nesiga occurs in a construct phrase modified by an attributive in Song

4.13  It is clear that nesiga is the usual result of the close relationship of two
nouns which form a construct phrase. Nesiga fails to occur only where such a
phrase is closely related to what follows. Presumably where such a relationship
exists, the phrase is not followed by the pause necessary for the development
of nesiga. Such relationship evidently exists between the parts of a short verbless
clause (#4.5), where the phrase stands in a constituent preceding the verb it
modifies (#4.6), where it is first of two parallel items followed by a significant
division (#4.7), and where the phrase is followed by modifiers (#4.9, 10, 11).
Where nesiga occurs in such situations, the main conditioning factor seems to
be the length of the unit formed (nesiga is less likely if the phrase forms part
of a short unit, particularly one of three words), and its position (nesiga is less
likely if the phrase and its modifier begin a major unit or a clause). In some
cases relationship to what precedes may also have influenced the development
of nesiga (#4.6).

In a few cases there seems to be no significant difference between the si-
tuations in which nesiga does occur and those in which it does not. It is possible
that the development of nesiga is conditioned in some such cases by the lexical

— 72 —

NESIGA

#4.13

relationship of the words in the context. In some cases the development may
have been prevented by the structure of the word (#4.2, 4.3). It is also pos-
sible, however, that the failure to occur may be simply anomalous, and this
seems very likely where nesiga fails to occur at the end of a c!ause Or major
unit.

5.1  . The participles treated in this chapter are those marked as absolute, and
those in which the same form is used in all situations. The majority of parti-
ciples included here are singular forms from the qal stem: mostly masculine,
a few feminine with the qames-he ending. Forms marked as construct are inclu-
ded in # 4. Few other forms show structure typical for nesiga.

5.6
It is clear from this description that, in the Twenty-One Books, nesiga
is common only in participles which end in a vowel, and only before nouns.
These conditions are independent: nesiga occasionally occurs in a form which
ends in a vowel but is not followed by a noun ^5.2.11, 5.3.11, 5.4.11), and oc-
casionally occurs in a form which is followed by a noun, but which does not
end a vowel (# 5.2.1b, 5.3.1b, 5.4.1b, 5.5.1b). There is some evidence that special
phonological conditions are required for nesiga to take place in this latter situ-
ation (it is most common where the participle ends in a labial consonant, and
the following word begins with a guttural), but there is not enough evidence
to be certain of this, or to state the conditions precisely. In the Three Books,
nesiga appears to occur in a participle under any phonological conditions where
it is followed by a noun, unless the noun is first of two short modifiers.

— 80 —

NESIGA

#5.7

5.7
It appears from # 5.4.1 that relationship of the participle to the preceding
or following word it modifies has some effect on the development of nesiga. It
is possible that nesiga fails to occur in a participle to which the definite article
is prefixed (#5.2.1, iii, 5.3.1) because the attributive relationship of the parti-
ciple to the preceding word prevented its forming an association with the fol-
lowing word close enough for the development of nesiga. However it is clear
that the presence of an anaphoric element following the participle, and relating
it to what precedes, is not conducive to nesiga, since nesiga occurs only rarely
before pN, and never before Av.

5.8  There is also evidence suggesting that the length of the phrase of which
the participle forms a part is significant for the development of nesiga (# 5.5.1a).
However it also appears from # 5.5.1a, that nesiga may be induced in a common
 ) more readily than in an uncommon one, and this may
phrase (
well be a factor in the regular occurrence of nesiga in the phrase

ט לי ף ר1 ר ב
.

השע ןנח ד

5.9
It appears from this that, apart from syntax (nesiga is common only in a
participle followed by a noun) the main factors conditioning the development
of nesiga in participles are phonological and rhythmic. Nesiga occurs where the
combination of sounds at the end of the participle and the beginning of the folio-
wing word is conducive to the joining of the two words in a single unit. Nesiga
also occurs under conditions which require —or at least facilitate— the treat-
ment of the participle and the following word as a closely-knit unit. Thus nesiga
occurs in a participle (as in a verb or infinitive, # 2.49.111, 3.6.1c) much more rea-
dily where second of two modifiers is long, allowing the division of the phrase
into two more or less equal units, than where it is one word only, so that the
phrase is likely to be divided after the participle, if at all. The suggestion that
a participle is not usually closely joined to the following word is borne out by
the fact that participles rarely show segol or patah in a final closed syllable, even
where that syllable is not stressed. This contrasts sharply with the situation in
verbs and infinitives (see # 9.4), and also in construct nouns of two or more sylla-
bles with original short *i in a final closed syllable. The vowel change in these
other forms undoubtedly reflects the fact that the word in question was closely
joined to the following word in speech, reflecting the close syntactic relationship.
The relationship to the following word of a participle which was not marked
as construct was not reflected by such close-joining in speech, unless the situation
was particularly conducive to it for reasons of speech rhythm. Consequently, alt-
hough nesiga is common in the construct forms of participles, as in other construct
nouns, it is not common in participles which are not marked as construct.

— 81 —

6.  Nesiga. in Verbless Clauses

6.1  A verbless clause is usually composed of two constituents, neither of
which is verbal, forming a «nexus of predication». There is a tendency to keep
this nexus as simple as possible; frequently it consists of two words only. In or-
der to achieve this, a constituent composed of a word with modifiers may be
divided, and the modifiers postposed after the nexus, as with the pN modifying
the noun forming the first part of the clause in

3:15

clause acting as noun substitute in

6.6
It appears from this description that the two parts of a verbless clause
typically do not provide a situation conducive to nesiga, Nesiga may occur in
this situation, but only when specific conditions are met, at least in the Twenty-
One Books. These conditions are (i) rhythmic, and (ii) phonological.

(i)  Nesiga can occur only where the two words forming the typical situ-
ation form a closely-knit unit. Where the two words form the nexus of predica-
tion of a verbless clause, such a situation occurs only where the two words
stands at the end of a clause which is not related to what follows, or where
the two words forming the nexus are followed by two or more elements forming
part of the same clause.

(ii)  Where the first condition is met, nesiga can occur only where the
first of the two words ends in a vowel, providing the situation most conducive
to the close-joining of the two words (see #5.6).

6.7  While nesiga does not occur where these conditions are not met, it may
also fail to occur where they do appear to be met. There may well be some
cause of the failure of nesiga in such cases which does not appear in the written
text, but there seems to be no feature of the evidence available which could
plausibly be offered as the cause of the failure of nesiga where both conditions
are met in the examples in # 6.4.1a. Even if it were argued that the presence
of a pronoun in both parts of the clause was conducive to the close-joining of
the two words, the failure of nesiga to occur in a number of cases would remain
unexplained. There is, in any case, no independent support for the suggestion.
The unusual wording of both the passages in which nesiga occurs in # 6.4.1b
makes any attempt at explanation hazardous.

— 87 —

#6.8

E. J. REVELL

6.8
In the majority of cases in the Twenty-One Books in which nesiga fails
to occur where the two words do not stand at the end of a clause, they are
followed by a single words, as in Gen 12:14, quoted in #6.5.1. There is plenty
of evidence that nesiga is unlikely to occur in the first word of a three word unit
(#2.54, 3.6.1c, 4.10, 5.5.1). In Rut 3:10 also quoted in #6.5.1, where nesiga fails
to occur, the accents mark the first three words as a unit, with a conjunctive
accent on the second the two words formings the nexus. This is also true of
Song 4:4, whereas where nesiga does occur, the corresponding word has a dis-
junctive accent. It could be argued, then, that in these cases, the accentuation
reflects the same grouping of the words as gave rise to nesiga. However the ac-
centuation does not always correlate with the occurrence of nesiga in this way,
either in the other cases quoted in #6.5.1, or where the two words are followed
by a single constituent of more than one word, as in Jer 32:43.

6.9
It is clear that nesiga occurs more readily in verbless clauses in the Three
Books than in the Twenty-One. It seems probable that the special characteris-
tics of the reading of the Three Books were particularly conducive to the close-
joining of the two words forming the nexus of predication. That is, a pair of
words was more likely to be spoken as a closely-knit unit, and to be separated
from what followed, than was the case in the reading of the Twenty-One
Books. Surprisingly, however, this effect seems to be reduced where the two
words forming the nexus are followed by a modifier or extraposed element of
two or more words.

— 88 —

7.1  This chapter includes the remaining situations in which nesiga occurs.
These are noun phrases of various sorts not discussed above, cases where the
two words forming the situation are a preceding modifier and the following
word it modifies, and cases in which the two words have no direct syntactic
relationship.

also Gen 18:24, 24:36, 47:I7, Ex 35:2, Lev 20:27, 26:5, 27:14, Nu 20:8, 21:2,

46:4, 47:5, 55:4, 69:12, 76:4, 77:4, 16, 78:24, 81:8, 83:5, 88:18, 89:23, 46,
105:17, 134:2, 147:15־ Job 3:7, 15, 20, 13:13, 18:9, 22:18, 34:37, 37:12, 38:39,
Prov 22:23, 24:27, 25:4, Qoh 9:6, Est 9:12, 1C 28:4, 2C 1:6, 24:5.

7.8  These examples provide the final demonstration that a close syntactic re-
lationship between the two words forming the typical situation was neccesary
for nesiga to occur. Linguistic rhythm produces the same result (the close joi-
ning of the two words, resulting in nesiga) only in the case in Nu 24:22. This
is the only case in the Twenty-One Books in which the words in question stand
at the end of a major unit. In the Three Books, they often stands in this posi-
tion. It is possible, then, that this is a further example of the fact that the rea-
ding of the Three Books affects nesiga differently from the reading of the
Twenty-One. It is also possible, however, that the case in Nu 24:22 is simply
an anomaly.

7.9  The survey of the diverse material in this chapter, with few examples in
any one category, adds little new to our knowledge of the conditioning of nesi-
ga. It does, however, confirm the view that nesiga results from close syntactic
relationship between the two words forming the typical situation (see #7.8).
It also shows again that nesiga occurs most readily where the two words forming
the typical situation are not closely related to what follows (# 7.3), and where
they are not unusually closely joined to what precedes (#7.3.11). The descrip-
tion also confirms that nesiga occurs most readily in a word ending in a vowel
(# 7-2-i, ϋ, 7·5·ΐ, 7-6)· Finally, there is indication that nesiga will not readily oc-
cur in the first words of a three-word unit, but may occur in a longer unit of
the same structure (# 7.5.Ü).

— 94 —

8.  Retraction of the Stress in Situations not Typical

for Nesiga

8.1  The way that nesiga occurs in the typical situation has been described
in detail in chapters 2-7. In addition to this material, there are a few cases in
which a «mga-like phenomenon occurs in a pair of words of which one does
not fit the typical situation. These are not considered to be examples of nesiga
for purposes of this study, but some, at least, are clearly related to nesiga, and
shed some light on the place of that phenomenon in the history of the language.

Perfect Forms with Waw «Consecutive»

8.2  Stress was not originally final in verb forms which end in a vowel. Final
stress developed in many such forms where they stood within speech units, but
this development is standard in MT only where the penultimate syllable was
originally short (open with a short vowel). Where the penultimate syllable was
originally long, final stress has developed in the Hebrew of the Tiberian text
only sporadically, except in first person singular and second person maculine
singular forms of the perfect with waw consecutive. In these forms, final stress
is usual, but has still not developed in MT in a number of situations, as de-
scribed in Revell, 1986. One of the situations in which stress is often penulti-
mate is that in which a form of this sort is joined by a conjunctive accent to
a word which is stressed on its first syllable, and acts as a constituent of the
clause. Penultimate stress occurs in these forms because the shift of the stress
to final position has been prevented. This is a process different from the retrac-
tion represented by nesiga, but it seems likely that the two processes are condi-

— 95 —

# 8.2

E. J. REVELL

tioned by much the same factors. The verb begins the clause in the examples
listed below, except in the case described in # 8.7.

8.3  The Verb is Followed by One Constituent.

(i)  Where the clause ends a major unit, stress is penultimate in Ex 33:14, Nu
23:3, Dt 17:14, 26:1, 27:7, 1S 19:3, 1K 22:13, Ez 28:22, Hag 1:9, 2C 18:12'.
Nesiga would occur according to # 2.7.

(ii)  Where the clause begins a major unit, stress is final in the combination
V — No in Lev 24:5, where the following clause has the same subject and ob-

ject. Nesiga would not occur according to # 2.8.ii, iii.

(iii)  Where the clause stands within a major unit, stress is final in the combi-

and is closely related to what follows in Dt 23:14, 1K 8:46 = 2G6:36. Nesiga
would not occur according to # 2-9-iiib. Stress is also final in Dt 7:25, before
a clause introduced by 13 «lest» which is closely related to what precedes.
Nesiga would be expected according to # 2.g.iiia.

(iii)  Where the clause stands within a major unit, stress is final in the combi-

to the following than those in which nesiga occurs in the same situation
(#2.13.11). Stress is also final the combination V — pN in Lev 19:34. This is

8.7
In Lev 26:36, the verb is preceded by an extraposed element, but waw
consecutive is nevertheless used. The verb has penultimate stress. Nesiga would
be expected in this situation in a verb which does not begin the clause accor-
ding to # 2.38.

8.8
(i) This survey shows that, where nesiga would occur in a verb form in
a typical situation, penultimate stress generally occurs in a perfect form with
waw consecutive which is joined by a conjunctive accent to a word stressed on
its first syllable. Where stress position does not correspond in members of the
two groups, it is usually not consistent in that particular situation in members
of either group. That is, the main factors which condition stress position are
balanced, so that minor differences in the context determine whether stress is
final or penultimate in any given form. Thus it is highly probable that stress
position in the two groups of forms was conditioned by the same factors.

(ii)  Where stress position does not corresponded in members of the two
groups in the same situation, perfect forms with waw consecutive usually show
final stress where nesiga is expected (#8.3.111, 8.4.111, iv, 8.5.1, ii, iv, 8.6.1, iii).
A waw consecutive form shows penultimate stress where nesiga is not expected

— 98 —

NESIGA

# 8.8.ii

only in three cases (#8-4.iv, 8.6.iii). This could be explained by the fact that,
where the penultimate syllable is closed, the situation is not typical for nesiga,
so that if stress were final in such forms, it would not be affected by the con-
ditions which induce nesiga. It is interesting to note, however, that, despite the
conclusion that final stress is more likely to develop in waw consecutive perfect
forms where the penultimate syllable is closed, and where the verb form is fol-
lowed by a word beginning with a guttural2, final stress does occur, where nesi-
ga is expected, in forms with an open penultimate syllable which are not followed
by a word beginning with a guttural (1S 2:35, #8.5.11; Ex 26:33, # 8.6.1; Ex
28:17, # 8.6.iii). This does seem to suggest that the factors inducing final stress
in these forms were stronger than those which gave rise to nesiga.

Forms in which the Penultimate Syllable was Originally Closed by a
Guttural

8,9  Where the penultimate syllable of a word was originally closed by alef,
he, het, or cayin, the letter in MT may be pointed with a composite shewa, simple
shewa, or a full vowel. Where a composite shewa is used, and the final syllable
is open, as in השעי , the structure of the word (CVCgCV) is clearly typical
for nesiga. Consequently such words are included in the description in # 2-7.
Where the guttural has a composite shewa, but the final syllable is closed, as
in ומאמ , the structure of the word is not typical for nesiga. Where the guttu-
ral has simple shewa, as in ·ליחי ,it is to be assumed that it represents «silent
shewa»*, so that here also the structure of the word is not typical for nesiga. The
same is true where a full vowel is used, as in ·ומרענ . Despite the fact that
the last three structures described are not typical, they may show penultimate
stress before a word stressed on its first syllable, as described below. All cases
noted in which words of such structure are joined by a conjunctive to a word
stressed on its first syllable have been included in this description except for
 or היהת . Stress in these forms is always final, even in the few
cases of

8.16  In the examples, then, stress position in the non typical structures consi-
dered is consistent with the occurrence of nesiga, or its failure to occur, in words
of typical structure, except in situations in which the occurrence of nesiga is
itself not consistent. Thus stress is penultimate in the example in # 8.11 in a
situation in which nessiga is not expected in the Twenty-One Books, but does
occur in the Three. In #8.12.1, stress is final in one case, penultimate in
another, in a situation in which nesiga is expected, but does not occur if the
clause in question is not particularly closely related to what precedes.

8.17  There is one other case in which stress is retracted in a word of which the
structure is not typical, in a situation which is otherwise typical for nesiga:

CVC3CV, as
*ni- r^—mu for ומרענ , and —CVCVG, as *way-ya-giq for קזחיו . Guttural
sounds were lost in all positions in the Samaritan pronunciation of Hebrew , and
in some forms of Galilean Aramaic . There is evidence of partial loss in the He-
brew materials from Qumran . It is, therefore, quite possible that a Jewish bibli-
cal reading tradition showing loss of guttural sounds in some positions should
have been current in Palestine. It seems most likely that this small group of ex-
ceptional cases reflects such a tradition. The questions that this raises for the his-
tory of the Tiberian tradition cannot, however, be discussed here.

8.21  Penultimate stress in the forms listed in #8.18-20 seems, then, to repre-
sent adjustment of stress pattern to fit prefered linguistic rhthyms, a phenome-
non of which nesiga is the most common type. These other examples present
quite different conditions from those which give rise to nesiga. The basic condi-
tioning factors are no doubt the same suprasegmental contours which give rise
to nesiga, but their effect differs according to the differing conditions, and there
are not enough examples to provide good information. The conditioning of pen-
ultimate stress in perfect forms with waw consecutive (#8.3-6) is clearly very
close to that which gives rise to nesiga. Stress on a penultimate syllable origi-
nally closed by a guttural (# 10-15) could have been conditioned in the same
way as was nesiga, at a stage at which the guttural had no consonantal value.
Since other forms of w^zga-like stress shift do occur, however, certainty on this
point is impossible.
