9.  Conclusions and Implications

Nesiga as a Phonological Phenomenon

9.1  The description in # 2-7 shows that nesiga is, to a very large extent, condi-
tioned by observable features of syntactic and other semantic relationship. The
latter should include the lexical relationship, to each other and to the surroun-
ding context, of the words forming the typical situation. This relationship
undoubtedly had some influence on the development of nesiga, but cannot be
objectively described. The other important factor which is not accessible to ob-
jective description is that here called «rhythmic». This aspect of the intonation
contours must have been the inmediate cause of nesiga. On one level it requires
the adjusment of stress patterns, under certain conditions, where one main
word stress would follow inmmediately after another. On another level, this
rhythmic factor governs the division of the text into the phrase-level speech
units at the end of which nesiga occurs.

9.2  The existence and significance of these phrase level units is not revealed
only by nesiga. There are several other types of binary variation which produce
contrasting «medial» and «terminal» phenomena, as does nesiga. These range
from the construct and absolute forms of nouns, to the contextual and pausal
forms of verbs, as described in Revell, 1986. The use of final stress in verb forms
from hollow roots, as in the two cases mentioned in # 1.14, is a further exam-
pie . Both those cases occur in the combination V — Ns in a clause of three

1

1 This statement is, of course, not based on these cases alone. A paper on stress position in these forms

has been prepared for publication.

— 105 —

# 9.2

E. J. REVELL

one-word constituents at the beginning of a verse, a situation in which nesiga
is not expected according to # 2.12.1. Thus the factors which condition nesiga
here are the same as those which condition final stress in such verb forms, just
as the same factors condition stress position in perfect forms with waw consecu-
tive.

9.3  Nesiga is, then, one example of the way in which stress position and vowel
pattern were affected by the intonation contours of the formal reading of the
text, giving rise to contrasting forms used within, and at the end of, phrases
or larger units. Many details have been left unaccounted for in the attempt
to describe the conditioning of nesiga, as is probably inevitable in a study invol-
ving the intonation contours of a dead language. No doubt much more can
be learned, however, particularly if nesiga is studied not as an isolated pheno-
menon, but as one of a group of related phenomena reflecting the influence
of these intonation contours.

9.4  The description shows that nesiga results from the treatment as a closely-
knit unit of speech, of the two words forming the typical situation. That is, nesi-
ga is a form of sandhi —a morphophonemic change under which the stress (and
vowel) pattern of the word spoken in isolation is adjusted to fit the pattern cha-
racteristic of a larger unit. Nesiga occurs frequently and regularly in verb forms,
infinitives, and construct nouns. This suggests that the syntactic bond between
a word of one these classes and the following word was naturally reflected in
speech by the joining of the two in a closely-knit unit. This suggestion is sup-
ported by the fact that members of these classes regularly retain patah (in con-
textual position) in a syllable which was originally open, but is closed, stressed,
and final in MT. This contrasts with the regular use of qames in such syllables
in participles and other nouns which are not in construct .2

9.5  Where the first of the two words forming the typical situation is a parti-
ciple, or is first word in a two-word nexus of predication, the spoken reflection
of the syntactic structure did not usually unite it with the following word in
a closely-knit unit. Such a unit is produced only where the intonation contours

2 The view that the absolute forms of nouns and the pausal forms of verbs reflect the same phonologi-
cal developments conditioned in the same way is not new. It is rejected in Blau, 1979, #4.1 (p. 13, with
reference to scholars who proposed it), apparently mainly on the basis that, in nouns of original form *CaC:,
qames appears in pausal forms, patah in contextual (referring to Blau, 1968, p. 37, n. 44). This is true of verb
forms of this structure, but not of nouns, unless the term «pausal» is used of position at the end of «phrase
level» as well as «clause level» units (as «terminal» in this study). The facts invoked thus do not support
Blau’s argument. See Revell, 1981, especially #6, p. 97; also Revell 1986, # 7, 8, 11:1.

— 106 —

NESIGA

#9.5

of the larger structure within which the situation occurs were particularly fa-
vourable to it. Even where this was the case, nesiga occurs only under particular
phonological conditions, of which the most important is that the first word of
the two end in a vowel (# 5.6, 6.6). These phonological conditions do not gene-
rally affect the development of nesiga in verb forms, infinitives and construct
nouns; their effect is limited to situations in which factors other than syntactic
semantic are conducive to nesiga, but not strongly so. This phonological condi-
tioning does not affect the development of nesiga in the Three Books either. Evi-
dently the method of reading employed in these books was such as to make nesiga
possible under all phonological conditions, even in participles and verbless
clauses. Very possibly the words forming a two word phrase or clause were ge-
nerally more closely joined to each other, and more sharply separated from
what preceded and followed, than in the reading of the Twenty-One Books.

9.6  The material in # 7, describing nesiga in various nominal structures, sup-
ports the suggestion in #9.4. Original short *a would typically give rise to qa-
mes in a closed stressed final syllable in the first word of the pair in all these
structures. Thus this word was not usually closely joined to the following. Con-
sequently, nesiga is virtually restricted to forms ending in a vowel. The only
significant exception seems to be the case of names (# 7.4.11). A two-word phrase
acting as a name would commonly be used in isolation, and so commonly be
spoken as a closely-knit unit. The fact that there are, among these structures,
very few examples of typical situations in which the first word ends in a conso-
nant is due to the fact that, in the majority of absolute nouns, the vowel of
a closed final syllable is qames. The most probable reason why stress is not re-
tracted from such syllables is that qames, in the closed stressed final syllable of
a noun form was long (cf. #1.12).

— 107 —

#9.7

E. J. REVELL

Thus, where the reflex is segol, nesiga always occurs. Segol does not occur in closed
stressed final syllables which were originally open; its appearance in a closed
final syllable shows that the vowel has remained short. The use of sere in such
a syllable does not show that the vowel is long4, but it must reflect the presence
of the conditions required for the lengthening of the vowel. These conditions
existed for 29 of the 30 participles which show nesiga (97 %), but only for 19
of 75 verb forms (25 %), and for 2 of 25 infinitives (8 %). Clearly the vowel
of a closed final syllable which was stressed when the form stood in isolation
was likely to be long in a noun, but not in a verb or infinitive. The length of
this vowel is premumably both the reason why nesiga is rare in nouns (other
than construct) which end a closed syllable, and a symptom of the conditions
which prevent its occurrence. Absolute nouns were not usually closely joined
in speech to the following word5.

א כ ז ב

9.8  Some support for this view is provided by the trasliterations of the second
column of Origen’s Hexapla. Original short *i in the final closed syllable of
a verb form is represented, in these transliterations, by epsilon in 13 cases (inclu-
 , Ps 89:36? in pause in MT). The only exception is הלה (Ps 32:8,
ding
also in pause in MT) in which eta is used. In the final closed syllable of a noun
form, eta is used in 21 cases, 10 of them participles. Epsilon is used only where
the syllable was originally doubly closed (8 cases), and in the participle  מ ל
(Ps 18:35), which may be construct. Elsewhere, epsilon is commonly used where
MT has hireq in an unstressed closed syllable. Eta is used where MT has sere
in a stressed open syllable, or reflecting an original diphtong *ai . These trans-
!iterations appear, then, to reflect a form of the language in which the develop-
ment of short *i has reached about the same stage as has that of short *a in
MT. The «short» reflex of the vowel is used in the final stressed closed syllable
of verb forms in contextual position, and of noun forms in which this syllable
was originally doubly closed, or (certainly for *a in MT, possibly for *i in the
transliterations) in the construct state. The «long» reflex is used in verb in pau-
sal position (again the evidence of the transliterations is not compelling), and
in other absolute nouns, including participles.

9.9  The transliterations of the second column show omicron where MT has
holem in a closed stressed syllable in verb forms, and in an originally doubly
closed final stressed syllable in nouns. Omega is used where MT has holem in
an open syllable, or elsewhere where it reflects an original long *ä or diphthong
*au. Omega is also used where MT has holem in the infinitive forms ö 1 לבל
and ברק (Ps 32:9) and טומב-ו (Ps 46:3). It is, therefore possible, although
far from certain , that the reflex of original short *u in closed syllables in verb
forms, and in originally doubly closed syllables in nouns, was still a short vowel
in the form of language reflected by these transliterations.

7

9.10  Nesiga occurs in a verb from with original short *u in a final closed syl-
!able in the two cases listed in # 1.14. Apart from these, the final or penultimate
syllable was originally doubly closed in most of the forms in which it might
have occurred (Ex 23:14, 37:3, 13, Dt 20:14, Ez 38:12, 13). Consequently the
failure of nesiga to occur night be due to the structure of the form. Exceptions
are 2) ליכיS 3:11) where nesiga would be unlikely (#2.18.iv), and רימאל
(Gen 23:5, 14), where nesiga would be expected (#3.2). If the use of omega in
comparable infinitive forms in the transliterations reflects a long vowel, as is
possible, the length of this vowel would be the cause of the failure of nesiga
to occur in these two cases in Gen 23:5, 14.

9.11  The conclusion that nesiga is prevented from occurring in words ending
in a closed syllable with short *u either by the length of the final vowel or by
the structure of the final or penultimate syllable is scarcely firm. However the
number of cases in which nesiga might be expected to occur in a form with ori-
ginal short *u in a final closed syllable, but does not, is too small to affect any
general conclusion. It seems probable, then, that nesiga could occur in a form
ending in a closed syllable only where the vowel of that syllable was short. If
so, nesiga can be described more accurately than it was in 1.12. Nesiga occurs
at the end of a «phrase-level» speech unit in a word ending------ CVCV,
------ CVC3CV, or, where the vowel of the final syllable is short,-------CVCVC,
where that word is closely joined to a word stressed on its first vowel.

7 For the infinitive forms, see Brönno, 1943, p. 56· The uncertainty is compounded by the fact that
omicron and omega did not represent different sounds in the Greek of Origen’s day (Gignac, 1976, p. 275-277),
so the fact that they appear to be used distinctly in the transliterations may be a coincidence. Origen may,
nevertheless, have used the signs to represent different Hebrew sounds, as «k» and «q» are used by English
speakers nowadays.

9.12  The use of qames on conjunctive waw occurs at the end of phrase level

In these other situations, the word preceding that with the prefixed conjunc-
tion has a disjunctive accent in 108 of 122 cases (89 %). Thus the qames does
not result from any particularly close relationship between the words joined
by the conjunction, but from the fact that the conjunction is prefixed to the
last word in the phrase. That is, in this terminal position, the intonation con-
tours produced an unstressed open syllable immediately before the final word
stress in the phrase. This is characteristic for original short *a in other terminal
forms, as in absolute nouns, and also occurs with other vowels in forms such
 and הלכוא . Presumably such a syllable was necessary for the
as  ·ותרכת
full realization of the terminal contour.

 ו

9.13  An unstressed syllable immediately before final word stress in the phrase
is also produced through nesiga. The reason is no doubt the same as that for the
production of such a syllable in other terminal forms. In the case of nesiga, this
unstressed sylable is either open, or closed with a short vowel. Where a closed
syllable in this position contained a long vowel, stress was not retracted. Possibly
the rhythm of the language could not accept an unstressed ultra-long syllable.
Possibly both the penultimate word stress in the phrase, and its terminal con-
tour, could be adequately realized in this situation. Retraction to a closed penul-
timate syllable was premumably also foreign to the rhythm of the language.

The Place of Nesiga. in the History of the Language

9.14  Nesiga seems to have developed at a time when the vowel and stress pat-
terns of the reading tradition had reached more or less the form represented
in MT, so that vowel change is not often involved, and features of structure
characteristic of earlier periods, but not of MT, rarely appear to affect the pro-
cess. There were some differences. Stress position in perfect forms with waw
consecutive was evidently not fixed when nesiga developed (#8.8), words with
a penultimate syllable originally closed by a guttural evidently differed both

8 See Revell, 1986, # 11.1.

# 9.14

from their original form and from that of MT (#8.17), and the accent system
was not fixed in the text in its present form (#1.15). These features offer no par-
ticular evidence for the date of the stage at which nesiga developed, but some
idea of date can be gained from other forms.

If this were not so, this vowel would have been stressed through nesiga as it
is in the pausal form, as יגרה (Ez 23:10), cf.

rials from Qumran commonly show waw in such forms where the syllable origi-

and Rut 2:8) by the time these transliterations were made13.

9.17  Nesiga occurs in a participle ending in a guttural with/>atoÄ «furtive» and
followed by a noun in 4 of 13 possible cases (31 %)
, about the same proper-
tion as in participles ending other consonants (29 of 82 cases, 35 %). To the
extent that patah. «furtive» represents some additional vocalic feature, whether
a second peak to the syllable, or a separate syllable, nesiga could not have oc-
curred when it was present. No corresponding vowel is represented in the transli-
terations of the Hexapla , nor in the Palestinian pointing, although it is by
no means certain that it would have been represented in either had it been
present. The use of the corresponding vowel before a final guttural in the Baby-
Ionian tradition occurs under quite different circumstances . Consequently
the Tiberian use of patah «furtive» was not a feature of the biblical tradition
before the Babylonian tradition began its separate development, and possibly
not before the developed of the Palestinian writing system. Nevertheless this
vowel is so far developed in the language of Qallir that it can serve as a
rhyme in his poetry . The difficulty of dating this poetry (probably sixth cen-
tury) and the fact that the language of Qallir is not that of the biblical tradi-

NESIGA

#9.17

tion, make this information difficult to use. It does show, however, that nesiga
must have developed some considerable time before the final establishment of
the vowel patterns typical of the received text.

18

9.18  The history of the biblical tradition, as far as it is known, supports this
suggestion. As far as the evidence shows, pausal forms, qames on conjunctive
waw, and nesiga, are all represented in biblical manuscripts with Palestinian
pointing with no more variation from L than may be found in other Tiberian
manuscripts . Consequently these must have been features of the tradition be-
fore the different writing systems were developed. The same appears to be true
for pausal forms and conjuntive waw with qames in the Babylonian tradition,
although a comprehensive study is still needed . Nesiga can only be marked
in those «Tiberianized» forms of Babylonian pointing which use Tiberian signs
for conjunctive accents, as P . However the fact that nesiga appears to reflect
the same conditioning factors which gave rise to pausal forms and to qames on
conjunctive waw suggests that all three features may have been characteristic
of the common stream of tradition from which the Tiberian and Babylonian
forms both developed. This does not offer any basis for a precise dating of the
period at which nesiga developed, but it does suggest that a date closer to the
time of Origen is more likely than one more distant. The development can,
then, tentatively be assigned to the third century of the current era .
21

20

19

Nesiga. and Other areas of Study

9.19  The above discussion shows that nesiga has considerable importance for
the the understanding of the language both from a synchronic and from a
diachronic viewpoint. It is doubtful whether anything has been said which is
directly relevant to any feature of ancient poetry (# 1.4), although nesiga does
provide evidence on how the poetical texts (and also the prose texts) were un-
derstood in the period when nesiga developed. Furthermore, relevant linguistic

18 See Revell, 1986a.
19 This statement is based on study of the editions of books of the bible with Babylonian pointing
which have appeared in the series Textes y Estudios. See under Biblia Babilonica in the list of works cited (p.
117 below). Further material is now available in Yeivin, 1985. See # 18. T —20. T, p. 255257־.

20 On nesiga in manuscripts with Babylonian pointing, see Yeivin, 1985, #17. T , p. 255.
21 This view coincides generally with the range of 200-600 suggested in Morrow and Clarke, 1986,
for the use of the dialect represented by the spelling of the qere forms in the Aramaic parts of the Bible.
These conclusions were originally presented by Prof. Clarke in a paper read before the SBL meeting in Sala-
manca in August 1983. I am most grateful to the authors for communicating to me their final conclusion
before its publication.

— 113 —

#9.19

E. J. REVELL

features can only be reached through reconstruction based on known facts, and
nesiga is certainly among the facts which must be considered in any attempt
to reconstruct the prosodic features of ancient Hebrew.

9.20  The conclusion that nesiga is a conditioned feature (even if the conditio-
ning cannot be fully described) leaves the argument that stress is phonemic in
Hebrew (# 1.3) untouched. It must be recognized, however, that this argument
is weak in the extreme. The claim that stress position is distinctive no doubt
referred, in its original form, to particular pairs of forms, not to the system as
a whole. Thus Rashi makes this claim in his interpretation of Gen 46:26-27,
where האבה appears once with final and once with penultimate stress, but
does not raise the question in Lev 18:28, where  ןל הא  is universally taken as
a perfect verb form, despite its final stress. On the level of the system, however,
stress position is genuinely distinctive only where the forms are considered in
isolation. In context, ·ונב «they built» does appear with penultimate stress
(Ez 27:5), and so is not distinguished from רנב «in us». It could be argued
that penultimate stress in the first case remains distinctive because it is con-
ditioned, since  ב נ יל  «in us» does not occur in a position in which its penulti-
mate stress could result from nesiga. It is, however, manifestly possible for a pN
phrase of this sort to be joined by a conjunctive accent to a word stressed on
its first syllable (as Isa 45:14, 50:2). Other pairs of homographs which have
different stress positions in isolation certainly show the same stress positions in
context under what appears to be the same conditions as
׳יער  (Dt 15:9, 2S
19:8, root עער ) and ״יערו Jer 50:19, Ez 34:23, root הער ). One of the
main contributions of this study is to emphasize the fact that stress position is
quite variable in Hebrew, and the even more significant fact that the variation
is conditioned at phrase level, not at word level. The subject needs more study,
but it nevertheless seems clear that if questions of phonemic status must be
asked of a written text, the prudent answer about that of stress position is nega-
tive.

ן

9.21  The final question concerns the relation of the stress position resulting
from nesiga to that of earlier stages of the language (#1.2). This has already
been answered in the conclusion on the date at which the phenomenon developed
(#9.18). It is true that the syllable stressed through nesiga was, in many cases,
originally antepenultimate, and may have been stressed at some early stage of
development. The fact remains, however, that these syllables have not been
stressed at any time after the loss of final short vowels, and the difficulties faced

— 114 —

NESIGA

# 9.21

by the argument that stress has, in particular forms, been preserved in the posi­
tion characteristic of so early a period are enormous. Why (for instance'! should
stress be retained through nesiga on the initial open syllable of
, but
not on the initial closed syllable of > even though these forms originally
had the same structure, and elsewhere stress moves forward most readily from
a short vowel in an open syllable? The fact that historical features of structure
have little discernible effect on nesiga is in itself adequate reason to believe that
this development took place in a form of the reading tradition closely similar
to that represented in MT, unless formidable arguments are marshalled
against it. Consequently it cannot be held that nesiga reveals the stress patterns
of an early stage of the language. All that this argument suggests is that the
overwhelming concentration on the reconstruction of the originals from which
the forms are derived, has led scholars to overlook a number of features of MT
itself, which provide information valuable for the study of the history of the
language.
